It’s the Cruelest Conspiracy on the Internet. Donald Trump Has Taken It Up. After a Dark Year, I Found the Truth.
2026-09-22

It’s the Cruelest Conspiracy on the Internet. Donald Trump Has Taken It Up. After a Dark Year, I Found the Truth.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
The video that sent me spiraling looks like a Fox News report. There’s President Donald Trump in a screen within a screen, sitting at the Resolute Desk. He smiles broadly as he signs an executive order. Then, b-roll, panning above a vast two-wing building identifiable as a hospital by its helipad. Next, an interior shot, sweeping across a gleaming white room occupied by sleek screens and glass-domed beds, space-age Sleeping Beauty contraptions. “Breaking now,” Lara Trump, the president’s daughter-in-law, says from what appears to be a Fox News studio, “President Donald J. Trump has announced a historic new healthcare system: the launch of America’s first medbed hospitals and a national medbed card for every citizen.” I saw this video the night Donald Trump posted it for his 10 million–plus followers. I watched it several times, genuinely shocked. The president of the United States had promoted one of the cruelest conspiracy theories on the internet: the dark fantasy of medbeds. The first thing to know about medbeds is that they aren’t real. Instead, they are the subject of an imagined plotline in which the military or some group of elites has access to extraordinarily advanced technology that can quickly and painlessly cure the human body of anything. In this fiction, peddled by right-wing conspiracy-theory influencers, the end to all disease and disability will arrive when Trump defeats his enemies and releases medbeds to the public—one of the many rapturous delights promised for that day. This conspiracy theory operates on a doomsday preacher’s ever-shifting timeline: The medbeds are always on the horizon, or being released imminently, or secretly already out there. There’s no good gauge of how many people believe in medbeds or Trump’s ability to deliver them. But a critical mass keeps the theory alive and rocketing around the internet. Trump’s post, I realized, had marked its entry into mainstream politics. Trump’s account deleted the video the morning after posting it. (We reached out to the White House several times for clarification on why but received no response.) But the damage had been done. This was September 2025, and A.I. videos were getting more convincing by the day. There was a telltale uncanniness to it—people clapping too slowly, as if underwater—but there was no disclaimer that it was fake. More to the point, the president had posted a video of himself speaking to the camera to make the announcement. It was easy to see how a viewer might think the report was real—or at least a nod to something real that was coming. At the time, I wrote a short article explaining that medbeds were another disturbing idea to come out of QAnon, the conspiracy theory that promises that Trump is secretly plotting to expose and arrest a cabal of evil elites trafficking children and poisoning society. I wrote about the president’s recklessness in giving this specific conspiracy theory his stamp of approval. After all, if someone thought a miracle cure was right around the corner, they might be more likely to stop taking their medication or skip burdensome treatments. The piece received a decent number of readers, and a couple of radio shows reached out to have me on to talk about Trump and his supporters who believe in medbeds. Then the internet, and everyone else, moved on. But I did not. I couldn’t stop thinking about medbeds. I found myself increasingly stuck on how nonsensical it all seemed, that such a patently absurd idea—magic beds!—could find its way to the most powerful person in the world. How, I wondered, had this happened? Had anyone involved in perpetuating it stopped to consider that it wasn’t harmless? That some people might stop taking insulin or pursuing chemotherapy, or that other desperate people would be given false hope? More than with any other conspiracy theory I had ever encountered, I needed to know who was responsible. What followed was a nearly yearlong obsession that, for stretches, took over my life. The search for the source of the medbed conspiracy took me into the world of scammers and crystals and alien invasions and broken families and sci-fi movie sets and the Arizona desert. I corresponded with con artists and attended a strange “medical” appointment that promised me invisible surgery and extraterrestrial matchmaking. I discussed lizard people and societal collapse with true believers. There was an entire month when I couldn’t stop talking to my editor about UFO discussion boards. I had grim conversations with many people about the horrors they’d seen after their loved ones fell into the world of medbeds. Somewhere along the way, I realized that my search for the origin of medbeds was about more than this one conspiracy theory. It was about how we came to such a dark moment in American life. In the end, I got answers—both about the origins of this specific conspiracy and about much more. And, after digging through layers of internet ugliness, I found the last thing I expected: a reason to believe we might just make it out of our current mess. It’s been about a year since Erika Looney decided she needed to cut her mother off. The moment came while she was driving to pick up her 8-year-old son, who has a developmental disorder. The school had called; he had hit another child. And then, sitting passenger in her Honda Accord as they went to get him, her mother said something that crossed a line: “It’s just a matter of time, and he’ll be completely fixed.” Erika knew exactly what her mother meant: Stop worrying, the medbeds are coming soon. She had heard it many times before, particularly on her son’s bad days. Erika understood why her mother, Bambi, would want medbeds to be real. It wasn’t just for her grandson. Bambi had lost a foot in a tractor accident when she was a child. But this time, Erika couldn’t bear it. Because of a rare chromosomal condition, her son will require full-time care for the rest of his life. With the way his disorder manifests, Erika’s family can’t do something as simple as go to the movies. She knows not to hope for a cure. And that day, Erika was too worn out and too upset to be patient anymore. “I flipped out, and I said, ‘I’m sick of this shit,’ ” Erika recalled. “I just shut down. And I think that’s really the moment I started blocking her out.” After that day, Erika stopped talking to her mother, even when she encountered her in her house. In May, she finally told her mother to move out. I heard a lot of stories like this as I dove into the world of medbeds. I found some of them in discussion forums. Other times, I was interviewing believers and, more often, their bewildered loved ones. People told me about parents who resisted treatment for cancer; a father-in-law who almost died of sepsis after neglecting a urinary tract infection; a father who, on his deathbed, complained that the medbeds weren’t there to save him; and an uncle who died after postponing medical care, sure that medbeds would soon arrive. These believers had, like so many conspiracy theorists before them, started from a place of legitimate suspicion of powerful institutions. They merely pushed further than the average internet truth seeker. On Facebook or Truth Social or X, even in texts from friends, they read about suppressed medical devices. They grew curious. They followed those threads to Facebook groups and Telegram channels. They were often, by this point, already convinced something big was coming that would upend our social order, if not our entire understanding of the world. And so, when they ended up in front of miraculous testimonials of healing, often supplemented by A.I.-generated photos or videos, they took it seriously. Now they crowdsource questions about when the beds will come and how they will work and argue over which medbed whistleblowers are real and which are frauds. They try to decode world events, looking for clues that the promised day of “awakening” has arrived. For Bambi, it was during the pandemic that she suddenly began talking about medbeds. Not long after, she started going on about Democrats eating babies. Later, Erika would realize that medbeds had been Bambi’s entry point into the world of right-wing conspiracy theories. Bambi, a woman in her late 60s, began consuming conspiratorial media so religiously that she set timers to make sure she didn’t miss podcasts. Eventually, she stopped taking her medication for high blood pressure and diabetes. Her blood sugar levels grew perilously elevated. A baffled Erika felt that her mom had been replaced with a combative and paranoid stranger. But to Bambi and those like her, medbeds are a source of comfort. The idea reassures believers that true patriots like themselves will soon be financially secure, respected, and healthy—and it also offers more immediate relief too. It provides a sense of community and the thrill of holding forbidden knowledge. In the Facebook and Telegram channels, believers share their stories of physical suffering and cheer each other on. Often, they post about loved ones with terminal diseases, trying to calculate whether the medbeds will arrive in time. “Any day now!” people write in the comments. They discuss what they’ll do first once they regain their vitality. Some fantasize about running around with their grandchildren. The daughter of another medbed believer recalled her mother’s words: We won’t have those aches and pains anymore; your dad’s heart will be better. “What’s heartbreaking,” the daughter told me, “is the hope.” But that hope, it turns out, is also profitable. The reply came within hours, and it was good news: With a refundable $500 deposit, I could secure an appointment with a medbed specialist in D.C. “Welcome to the Med Beds Technology Registration Department, where we facilitate access to cutting-edge quantum medical technology for health restoration and wellness enhancement,” the email read. In the forums, I had noticed some accounts insisting that medbeds were already being rolled out. Official-sounding email addresses floated around, offering bookings and stringing people along before asking for large payments. I reached out to an account I found in a Telegram chat that offered a medbed appointment. The reply came from a purported government agency, “the National Quantum Coordination Office.” I asked questions about the technology and how the process would work; it was firm in saying I could get answers only at the actual appointment, which I could reserve with a Venmo payment. I had found this email, and others, through accounts affiliated with a figure known as “Skye Prince.” There are numerous Skye Princes in the various medbed channels, but the original is a short-haired, ruddy-cheeked Australian woman named Kerrie-Ann Thornton, who claims to be a former medbed nurse with insider information. Though Thornton says on her website that the medbeds will be free, she and her impersonators also serve as a gateway to the many ways you can currently spend money on medbeds. There’s a Swiss company, for example, that sells “virtual medbeds”—$2,500 for nothing but the vague promise of DNA reprogramming. A U.S.-based chain of medbed operators called Tesla BioHealing has been flagged by federal regulators for claiming that its “Life Force Energy” treatments can cure stroke paralysis, epilepsy, Alzheimer’s, and terminal cancer. Some operators simply ask for fees to jump customers ahead on “waiting lists” when the medbeds do eventually arrive. I never sent any money to the National Quantum Coordination Office, so I never got the answers the appointment promised. But despite the typos and inconsistencies in the emails, I understood how such an unsophisticated scam might actually be effective if the mark was sufficiently confused—or frightened. I thought of one 64-year-old woman named Johna, with whom I had been emailing. Johna told me she believed that the medbeds would cure her metastatic breast cancer. She no longer has any trust in established medicine, not after watching the deaths of two of her own children, which convinced her that doctors intentionally keep patients sick to “keep the money coming in.” So when she discovered a medbed Facebook page two years ago, she was intrigued. She began corresponding with an official-sounding Skye Prince account on Messenger and filled out an application for a healing appointment. Following instructions, Johna bought a Vanilla Visa gift card, loaded it with $300, and prepared to pay her medbed appointment fee. As Johna was about to send her information over, she suddenly grew suspicious. She decided to question the account, and it eventually admitted that it wasn’t the real Skye but someone doing administrative work on her behalf. Johna backed out of the chat, realizing that she’d only just escaped being scammed. But the near miss didn’t scare her away from medbeds. Instead, she decided she needed to know more about the real Kerrie-Ann Thornton. She sought out other Facebook groups. She was impressed that the real Skye Prince was calling out the copycat accounts and that she insisted the beds would be free. “I believed her words to be genuine,” she wrote to me. There was, as I’d discover, a lot deeper to go. It is impossible to separate the rise of medbeds from the mutant growth of QAnon. QAnon, as many people think of it, is long gone; Donald Trump never fulfilled QAnon’s original promises, and the figure known as “Q” went silent years ago. But in the collapse, Q’s followers dispersed and attached themselves to new communities and interests, and today QAnon’s form is a vast tangle of fantastical and often contradictory storylines. The medbeds conspiracy theory is part of this mess. The QAnon DNA is right there: Somewhere secret, perhaps in deep underground bases, there are medbeds. Some nefarious force is keeping them from the public—or, alternatively, medbeds are being kept a secret by the Good Guys for nobler and more strategic reasons. Soon enough, though, Trump will reveal the beds, and a new age of vitality will begin. Sometimes, the medbeds promise patriots more than just healing: near immortality, or something even more dazzling. One QAnon account I found argued that medbeds would activate our 10 strands of dormant DNA, giving us telepathic abilities. One woman, whom we’ll call Anna, told me of an aunt who, like many medbed believers, carries on all the old QAnon ideas: a still-living John F. Kennedy, secret reptilians in the British royal family, Democrats who eat babies. Anna is staying with her aunt in hopes of persuading her to visit a doctor for severe neurological pain. Anna has instead been able to see how bad it’s gotten: Her aunt is so certain of the medbeds’ imminent arrival that she won’t seek medical help for a broken arm. Her aunt has converted $600 into worthless Iraqi dinars and Zimbabwean dollars that she thinks she’ll be able to use to get access to the technology when it becomes available. You can find similar Q-colored chatter of medbeds on X, Facebook, Rumble, Gab, Bitchute, Parler, and elsewhere. These kinds of discussions have been common on these platforms since 2022. But go back earlier, and the talk starts to dwindle. Before 2018, it disappears: The platforms are almost entirely void of the medbed conspiracy. Instead, when people spoke about medbeds, it was in the context of science fiction. Star Trek had do-it-all hospital beds called biobeds. The 2012 film Prometheus featured doctor-free MedPods. Most on the nose was 2013’s Elysium, in which healthcare is sporadic on a post-collapse Earth. On the Elysium orbital space station, Med-Bays (some of them Versace-branded) cure the superrich’s every ailment. I had for some time wondered whether Elysium had been the inspiration, but my suspicions were heightened when, upon watching, I discovered that the movie’s premise is a Matt Damon–led quest for healthcare access that ends with the Med-Bays auto-deploying to the masses. I even called Phil Ivey, Elysium’s production designer, at his home in New Zealand. He told me that a scam artist had once emailed him about a plan to sell sessions in a “wellness/Anti-aging device as introduced in the blockbuster movie ‘Elysium’!” Ivey had designed Med-Bays to look like real-world MRIs, after all. There have always been overeager people who, through either extreme wishful thinking or some quirk of brain chemistry, have come to believe that science fiction hides secret messages or covers up for real events. But for a long time, these had been isolated individuals, too few and sparse to have any kind of cultural imprint, and certainly lacking the clout to land a video in the social media feed of the president of the United States. But somewhere along the way, that changed. Understanding exactly how that happened and when is critical, and not just for my quest to get to medbeds’ patient zero. It helps us to understand the broader information nightmare in which we’re now living. There’s nothing new about vaccine trutherism, or a belief in a global cabal of elites, or faith in an all-curing snake oil. What’s new is that those theories have gained enough power to alter our daily lives: a Health and Human Services secretary undermining vaccine access for children; a president whose pardon policy is premised on the myth that the deep state stole the 2020 election; the world’s richest man insisting that elites are carting in immigrants to destroy the political power of white people. This is not the first age with conspiracy theories. We’re just living through a time when they’ve escaped containment. I thought discovering how medbeds had made the jump from science fiction to QAnon would help me understand. I needed to know if there was some cynical political operative or gleeful nihilist behind this mess. I wanted to find out if the conspiracy theory’s originator had known what they were doing, if they now felt delight at the heights their creation had reached. I became obsessed. So I threw myself down one rabbit hole after another. I spent weeks at a time poring over old social media posts. I worked with researchers who’d had the foresight to maintain archives of 4chan’s main politics channel. I listened to obscure podcasts and read hyperniche blogs. I spent fruitless hours testing my employer’s patience, neglecting my more immediate work while dredging up zero-retweet Twitter posts from the early 2010s. But it was YouTube that finally cracked it for me. The first medbed mentions, it turned out, came not in Trump-loving channels or via QAnon posters. Instead, they appeared in a different online enclave entirely: the New Age movement, which takes a more metaphysical approach to reality. Spiritually inclined YouTubers discussed medbed healing in videos about frequencies, crystals, and fifth-dimension consciousness. This talk had proliferated in these corners of the internet as early as 2015—three years before MedBeds landed in QAnon spaces. In these forums, one name kept coming up: Captain Randy Cramer. In one video I found, Cramer, a serious-looking man in his 40s, discussed “holographic regenerating medical beds” back in 2015. In it, he referenced comments made in 2014, though they seem to no longer exist on the internet. This was and still is the earliest recorded mention I could find. I was fairly certain I had identified the conspiracy theory’s starting point. I left Cramer a voicemail. Had he been the first to talk about medbeds? Would he be willing to speak to me about them? Two days later, I received a call. Yes, he said, he had been the first. And he was happy to talk. I bought a ticket to Sedona, Arizona, to meet Cramer in person. I arrived in Arizona with a heaviness hanging over me. I had seen so much ugliness online. I had heard so many stories of real-world harm inflicted on sick and elderly people. And here I was, headed to meet the man I believed had started it all. I was apprehensive. But Sedona, one of the great New Age capitals of the world, almost immediately shifted my perspective. Pink Jeeps shuttled tourists to healing vortexes. Every third shop stocked bins of crystals. A woman I met during a UFO tour said she had signed up for a “healing chamber” experience from a vendor that also offered telekinetic spoon-bending lessons. At a store selling harmonic healing devices, I watched a man cradle a swaddled Chihuahua within a 9-foot-tall golden pyramid structure in an attempt to heal the dog’s vertigo. In this context, medbeds seemed conceptually like fun oddities, akin more to palm readings than to predatory disinformation. I decided I had to try out a medbed for myself. I found Sedona Quantum Consciousness online, via an advertisement for its Cell-Well Medbed, which appeared in photos to be a souped-up tanning bed. This wasn’t the kind of medbed featured in QAnon chats and scam pages: These real-life “medbed” experiences provide significantly less spectacular promises and don’t claim to be using classified government information. Still, there were similarities. The bed I would try was actually a “tachyon chamber,” a device that, the owner assured me over the phone, was even more like a real medbed than the one I’d seen online. I was told it had healed diabetes sores, infertility, a frozen shoulder, and the common cold. It cost $75 for a 20-minute session. I met the owner, Ada Tourtsakis, at her yurt on the outer edge of Sedona’s civilization. I was ushered in and asked not to take any photos of the tachyon chamber, which occupied its own inner sanctum in the yurt and was the proprietary technology of a man known only as Cobra. The tachyon chamber turned out to be, essentially, a narrow room. On the floor was a single mattress, low to the ground, with carnation-pink satin sheets. Quartz crystals sat at each of the bed’s four corners and atop a small glass table to the side. A pyramid of gold-colored rods was situated over the bed. Tourtsakis had me lie down and shuffle upward until the pyramid’s center was over my hips. She turned off the light and closed the door, leaving me with instructions to be open-minded. For 20 minutes I lay alone, in the dark, mulling over what I’d been told. Tourtsakis explained it to me like this: The process relied on the crystals in the room, which were quantum entangled with particles out in space. Once activated, they created a kind of wormhole in the pyramid through which altruistic aliens from the star cluster Pleiades would emerge. The Pleiadians would then harness spiritual energy from subatomic tachyon particles in order to heal me, performing what Tourtsakis referred to as “surgeries.” Pleiadians, common figures in New Age cosmology, are largely spiritual entities that guide humans toward higher levels of consciousness. In medbed lore, they are one of several kinds of aliens cited to explain the super-advanced “dimensional” medical technology. Tourtsakis had told me that the Pleiadians were the ones who had decided that the pyramid’s center should be at my hips, identifying them as the source of my chronic knee pain. If I wanted, she said, they could also direct me to a new career or my soulmate. I asked if I needed to believe in them for these things to work. She said it didn’t matter. They would help anyway. The New Age movement isn’t entirely apolitical. Many New Agers do believe in a coming Trump deliverance, and there has therefore always been a New Age contingent within QAnon. Tourtsakis herself believes that Trump will deliver the real medbeds. But in practice, Tourtsakis’ operation felt very different from the version of medbeds I had found on the internet. She told me that most of her clients were tourists seeking spiritual experiences, not desperate sick people. I knew she wasn’t promising a cure for cancer. And though my knee pain hadn’t been fixed, I found the experience relaxing. This felt recreational—not dangerous. Still, I was aware how dark this could all get. As I walked out into the desert sunshine, I tried to reconcile the suffering I’d seen online with aliens performing metaphysical microsurgeries throughout my body. I was stuck, but only temporarily. Because the next day, I was going to meet the man who had started it all. Here’s what Randy Cramer says happened to Randy Cramer. In 1969, a secret branch of the U.S. Marines implanted an unwitting woman with a genetically modified embryo in order to produce one of its new class of supersoldiers. That secret branch then swooped in to train the resulting child—Cramer—starting around age 3 or 4. Cramer’s family was unaware of this middle-of-the-night training thanks to “trace amounts of time travel” that would send him right back to bed as if nothing had happened. At age 17, Cramer was sent off to fulfill his 30-year service in the Marines’ secret space program. Much of that time was spent in the Mars Defense Force, which protected Earth colonies on Mars. “It was pretty much just fight bugs, fight reptiles,” he said. Then, abruptly, he was returned to his childhood bedroom, a healthy 17-year-old again. This had been accomplished with time travel and cloning technology. And though he has now recovered the full story, at the time, his memory had been wiped. He was left with a vague feeling of waking from a very long dream. He moved through his late teen years with a nagging sense he was different from others. At around age 19 or 20, he began having regular night terrors and daily flashbacks to “horrific, nightmarish things.” This went on for almost a decade, until, with the guidance of a therapist, he diagnosed himself with post-traumatic stress disorder. He began a yearslong meditation regimen to find the source of that trauma. By his mid-30s, he had unlocked years of memories from his time fighting battles in space. One of the earliest memories he discovered was of his first medbed experience. It had come in the heat of battle on Mars, after a reptilian alien ripped his arm off. Medics whisked him to safety, gave him a pain blocker, and placed him in a bed with a recessed, plush mat that molded to his body. A mechanical arm emerged from the top, ending in what he described as a giant lamp head. It projected a 3D model of an arm onto the wound. The projection, through its extremely high resolution and particular frequency resonance, tricked his cells into new growth. After five hours of a painless electric tickling sensation, he had a fresh arm, and he was sent back out into battle. Cramer told me all this, with remarkable directness, in a lengthy interview in the basement where he records interviews for various UFO shows. Instead of his house, we were at a New Age event venue in a strip mall that also contains an apothecary, a dry cleaner, and a head shop. I had arrived to find Cramer waiting for me outside, smoking a joint, patient and pleasant. He turned out to be an ordinary-looking 56-year-old man with a buzz cut and salt-and-pepper beard. And he was, I was finally sure, the first to talk about medbeds online. Everything he said tracked with my understanding of the idea’s earliest use. He himself seemed certain no one had mentioned medbeds before him. No one seems to know who coined the term, but the label wasn’t what mattered; it was the idea that the military was hiding a device that could fix everything wrong with you. After discovering his suppressed memories, Cramer began teaching meditation classes for others seeking to unlock the mysteries of the mind. At first, he kept his extraterrestrial experiences to himself, he said, because he was afraid of the potential blowback. But one day, a person he calls “the brigadier,” who communicates through a device implanted in Cramer’s brain, reached out to tell him that the space Marines would support him going public. The military’s reasoning was that leaking some of the information about these interplanetary conflicts might reduce the shock to the public the day the real revelations came. And so Cramer became a whistleblower. In 2014, under the pseudonym “Captain K,” Cramer sat for a multihour interview for one of the bigger “exopolitics” YouTube shows. Suddenly, he was a known name in the secret-space-program community, as one of the initial such firsthand experiencers to come forward. He began giving presentations at UFO events and interviews on paranormal programs. From there, medbeds filtered out to the rest of the secret-space-program community and to other alternative subcultures, including the New Age world. Obviously, Cramer’s claims, which hang on cloning and time travel, are out there. And it would be easy to conclude that he’s running a kind of big con. Cramer has earned money from his secret-space-program tale, selling lessons on “how to make brains smarter, faster, more effective.” More troubling, in 2017 he started a GoFundMe to raise money for the first medbed prototype. But he doesn’t seem to be riding off medbeds now, in spite of how popular they’ve become. The GoFundMe made only $8,700, according to the archived page. He had decided it wouldn’t be feasible to make a medbed, and he moved on. The problem is that the world did not, which was partially why he’d agreed to talk to me. He wanted to distance himself from the scams that he was seeing—and from the way he had watched this one minor detail in his life story snowball. Cramer has been going to conferences related to extraterrestrial matters for years, and he knows that his audiences have always been “outside the mainstream.” But starting around 2015 or 2016, the people coming to his talks had gone, in his words, “nuts.” This was the period when Pizzagate, the proto-QAnon conspiracy theory, began to scramble our political realities. “It starts attracting everybody,” he said of the forums he participated in and the speaking events he attended. “You start getting all the anti-vaxxers in, the New Age people, a bunch of—I don’t know what to call them, other than quasi-deconstructionist neo-Christians. I mean, to them, Jesus was an alien.” He held particular scorn for the flat-earthers, who he thought were plants to discredit genuine research about extraterrestrial technology. “I’ve done my share of saying, ‘That’s bonkers,’ ” he said, “and it makes people go, ‘You’re a member of the deep state.’ ” I asked him about the A.I. video that Trump posted of medbeds. Cramer’s no fan of Trump regardless, but the president doesn’t know a thing about medbeds, he said. It was simply him “pandering” to an audience. (Cramer, though also no fan of Democrats, thinks Trump is a “terrible person.”) Cramer is all too familiar with the medbed fallout. He watched, helpless, as MAGA and QAnon fed his healing technology into the exploitative machinery of politics. In an earlier era, perhaps, Cramer’s leaks about Martian bases and wormhole gates would have remained within his insular world of UFO blogs. But instead, it spread wildly, devastatingly out of control. I asked Cramer if he regretted telling the world about medbeds. He didn’t hesitate. “Totally, yeah,” he said. “It would have been way better off never mentioning that was a thing, knowing where we are now. I would have shut my mouth and never given people rope to hang people with.” He has spent years living with the consequences. “People started dropping their really heavy medical conditions at my feet, going, ‘Oh my God, please, somebody, can you help me?’ And I would just be like, Oh, I so wish I could,” he said. “That really shifted how I felt.” A dozen or so times a month, he said, he still receives emails from people asking where they can get treatments. “Their emails start out, ‘You’re my last hope of not being a crippled, miserable, dead person,’ ” he said. “It’s just heartbreaking emails. And I have to tell them, ‘I’m really sorry. It’s going to be a waste of time. I’m afraid I don’t have the tech.’ ” I walked away from our conversation with the conviction that the man who started medbeds was neither trying to make money nor influence politics. He was, instead, a true believer. Specifically, Cramer believes that humanity is on the cusp of a new, better era. Cramer’s vision doesn’t require anyone to send him money or rely on Donald Trump. Instead, he thinks humanity can deliver its own salvation—albeit with a bit of inspiration from aliens. In his telling, our species will undergo rapid transformation once we understand what’s possible. We will stop obsessing over money and fighting over resources and instead organize our society around generosity and kindness. When we’ve done that, we’ll join the rest of the universe in exploring the stars. He thinks this next step is inevitable. And in a just world, that’s where this would have started and ended: Cramer living in the boundaries of his fun, space-loving subculture, meditating in the Martian desert landscapes of Sedona, not hurting anyone. But that’s not the world we live in. In the world we do live in, a guy named Randy in Arizona, who just wanted to talk about aliens’ love of Earth beer and the annoyances of space-military bureaucracy, instead ended up creating a conspiracy theory that has baby boomers on Facebook refusing to go to the hospital and begging Trump to heal their broken bodies. I left Cramer that day feeling both deeply fascinated by his story and slightly depressed. I’ve worked at Slate for almost an entire decade, writing frequently about fringe conspiracy theories from dark corners of the internet. And over those years, those dark corners of the internet have expanded to be just, well, the internet. The conspiracy theories are no longer fringe: The president of the United States posted a video about magic, cancer-curing beds, and it was routine enough that it’s entirely possible I’m the last person still regularly thinking about it. What Cramer’s story shows is just how ruthlessly efficient the system behind that change is. The internet, suffused with popular anger, drawing from a polarized society, and built with rapacious algorithms, can chew up the most joyful of ideas. Cramer, I felt, didn’t deserve this. It didn’t seem right that he should have to carry the weight of what more-cynical people did with his idea. But that night, I continued to reflect on the answer I’d gotten to my medbed mystery. The more I thought about it, the more I realized there was something comforting about my conversation with Randy. Given all the suffering I’d seen, I had expected my medbed search to end with a villain. But there had been no evil mastermind. Instead, the problem was the information ecosystem of our era. As Cramer’s vision moved from one subculture to the next, various algorithms—and the incentive structures of social media—ensured that the wacky and wondrous aspects would be cast off, remaking the idea into a tool for manipulation. And yet somehow, strangely, I see something hopeful in that. If each conspiracy theory evolved from a dark mind, if human cruelty were the driving force behind all of this, there would be little hope for us. But if the systems around us are what’s responsible for the chaos, those can change. Not quickly, and not easily, but—and maybe I sound like Cramer here—societies can evolve. The day after talking to Cramer, I returned to the New Age center for a final event before heading out. I joined six others on mats on the venue’s galactically swirled green-blue floors. Cramer was also in attendance, sitting off to the side. A woman sprayed each of us with rose essential oil to help with grounding. She said she had co-created it with a higher-frequency being. Our host, sitting alongside his “BioCharger” machine, prepared us for a session in which he would rid us of parasites inside the body and also parasites out in the world. (We might have an additional bowel movement the next day, he warned.) Inside the machine, which looked like a large blender with a metal doughnut inside, blue and orange lights vibrated within glass tubes, while feathery electricity danced out the top. It cycled through a range of staticky notes—the frequencies that would heal us. After the session, Cramer, who was a regular at these events, would get down on the floor to show me all the different chambers within the host’s device, eager to explain its settings and how it worked. This, Cramer would tell me, was true quantum science, not something that operated just “off vibes.” But before we got to that, during the healing itself, I kept looking over at Cramer. It wasn’t just the kind of hard science he liked to geek out about that was going to make the difference here—our guide had told us to focus on love and forgiveness as we were healed. That, he had said, was part of the process. Cramer, I knew, believed in the healing power of meditation. He had said it was how he had healed his PTSD. I drove out of Sedona with the picture of Cramer still in my mind, sitting on a pouf at the edge of the floor, rigidly upright, eyes closed, hands pointing down and steepled together, meditating on forgiveness and love. Trump Went So Far This Time That Even Fox News Won’t Stand For It
2026-09-21

Trump Went So Far This Time That Even Fox News Won’t Stand For It

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Liberals have long accused journalists of letting Donald Trump walk all over them, lying to reporters’ faces and using them as political punching bags even as he basks in the glow of their camera lights. But the press’s posture may now be changing. After Trump announced he was banning three news outlets from the White House, the major TV networks—including Fox News(!)—acted as one to protest the president’s decision. The dustup carries important implications for the First Amendment and journalists’ ability to tell the rest of us what’s going on inside this administration’s inner sanctum. With a Truth Social post, as Trumpian brouhahas often do. On Friday afternoon, the president put out a lie-filled, haphazardly capitalized post announcing he’d banned CNN, MS NOW, and Politico from the White House. Trump claimed the ban was “a result of their constant ‘reporting’ FAKE NEWS” and pledged that “Other Fake News Media Outlets” would get the same treatment. By Saturday, reporters from those three organizations were being denied White House access. CNN, MS NOW, and Politico jointly sued the administration, arguing that the ban “could not be a more direct assault on the First Amendment.” On Monday, the administration barred CNN from the “pool,” a rotating crop of TV journalists who film presidential events. So the other four major networks—ABC, CBS, Fox, and NBC—decided to suspend the pool indefinitely. Media outlets unaffected by the ban, like the Atlantic and the Washington Post, issued statements of solidarity. What’s lost when reporters aren’t allowed into the White House? In fairness, most reporters still are. Print, online, and radio journalists continued to follow Trump as normal today. But even though everyone has a camera in their pocket these days, the TV pool is how news outlets around the world get footage of presidential events in sensitive areas, like Trump’s Oval Office meetings with world leaders or appearances aboard Air Force One. Without it, the only footage the public will see from those places is what the White House wants it to. The administration has teased that it could soon replace poolers with its own video feeds, which would effectively amount to state television. “The public has a vital interest in receiving accurate, independent information about its government,” the five major networks said in a joint statement. “No administration should restrict a news organization because it objects to its reporting.” A federal judge named Timothy Kelly will hear the three banned outlets’ lawsuit. Trump appointed Kelly in his first term, but the judge sided with CNN in 2018 after the White House tried to yank a reporter’s press credentials. Kelly has scheduled a Wednesday hearing, which could make Trump’s ban short-lived. The president has preemptively vowed to appeal any adverse ruling. In the meantime, the ban is already having an effect. On Monday, Trump cut the ribbon to open his new White House helipad, but the TV pool was missing and the administration’s feed was inaudible, resulting in footage of the president speaking over the sound of static. Deciding which journalists deserve access to the White House isn’t an easy question. The press briefing room has a finite number of seats, and who should get them has been a contentious question under many administrations. But this is a naked effort to punish outlets for coverage the president doesn’t like—and it’s likely aimed at having a chilling effect. Being on site helps reporters cultivate sources and break news, which in turn helps inform those of us who don’t have access to the building. If covering the president aggressively risks getting your outlet banned, it pressures reporters to pull their punches or shy away from stories that could anger the administration. Trump has already used the bully pulpit and lawsuits to try to wring more sycophantic coverage out of the media. “There’s something wrong with a country that can allow people to write purposely negative stories,” he said in the Oval Office on Friday, shamelessly trampling the founders’ vision of a country in which a free press was sacrosanct. Trump’s ban is another attack on that freedom, and it’s unlikely to be the last. Looking for an evening escape from the latest erosion of our constitutional rights? My colleagues have some suggestions. Meet the men who still love Bitcoin: Cryptocurrency’s power has declined, but it still holds a powerful allure to some people disillusioned with America’s economic system. Nathaniel Moore went to Las Vegas to see why some lost boys are still pinning their hopes on Bitcoin. Read about a beloved author’s new novel: John Green, author of classic young-adult books like The Fault in Our Stars, hasn’t published a novel in a decade. Now, he’s back with his first-ever book for adults. Laura Miller writes that Hollywood, Ending is potent “enough to make you wonder why anyone ever thought his earlier books were just for kids.” Raise a glass to a classic cocktail: The margarita’s ingredients may be simple, but its origin story is far more complicated. N.C. Stevens digs into the drink’s history and tells a fascinating tale about Prohibition and the delicious outcome of cultural fusion between the U.S. and Mexico. Watch a scary new film: Video games are notoriously difficult to adapt to other media, but, according to Sam Adams, the new Resident Evil movie makes the leap from console to screen “with the right mixture of fidelity and irreverence.” And, if you’re a gamer but not a horror connoisseur, Nadira Goffe helpfully breaks down whether Resident Evil will be too scary for you, using Slate’s highly scientific Scaredy Scale. Whether you’re watching with excitement or hiding under the covers, I hope you have a great evening! See you tomorrow. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. ABBYY gives old-school OCR a job in the AI pipeline
2026-09-22

ABBYY gives old-school OCR a job in the AI pipeline

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
FineParser runs in a CPU-powered container, preserving tables and layout before handing documents to an LLM ABBYY has packaged its FineReader OCR engine as a self-hosted tool for turning documents into structured text that AI systems can use. The company's new FineParser runs in a Docker container on a CPU, without requiring a GPU. It aims to preserve the layout of a document as it extracts its contents. It takes images of documents, in multiple languages, and turns them into structured, formatted text – so that they can be processed using, for instance, modern generative AI LLMs. This isn't its sole purpose: the company suggested it could help bring print text into a modern CMS, or for archiving, or as a stage in some form of production pipeline. ABBYY describes FineParser's approach as "deterministic" AI. It extracts text and document structure rather than generating a plausible rendition of them. Its output can then be passed to a generative AI system, whose responses are less predictable. The company also offers its own programmable machine-learning framework, NeoML, which is FOSS and available on GitHub. It also publishes an OCR SDK for companies wanting to embed the FineReader engine into their own products. FineParser itself isn't open source, although ABBYY maintains a GitHub repository for examples and community support. The self-hosted tool has a free tier allowing 1,000 pages per month for one year. ABBYY says its subscription tiers connect to a license server for validation; a fully offline deployment requires an Enterprise plan. Preserving structure means recognizing columns in reading order, headings, and tables – even those without borders – rather than returning a jumble of extracted text. ABBYY says FineParser also handles handwriting and more than 200 languages. Developers can submit documents through FineParser's REST API and receive output as plain text, JSON, or DocLang, a compact format intended for LLM input. FineParser's pitch is that, before an LLM can make use of a document, something has to read it properly. ABBYY is betting that its decades-old approach to OCR – running on a CPU, preserving the layout, and leaving the text-generating to other tools – still has a place in the AI pipeline. Sometimes the old-fashioned part is the useful part. ® FineParser runs in a CPU-powered container, preserving tables and layout before handing documents to an LLM PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity BigDiskBuster leaves Microsoft's antivirus running but unable to install updates War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws Did Copilot write the synchronization code? NightmareEclipse's latest zero-day leaves Microsoft Defender stuck in the past
2026-09-22

NightmareEclipse's latest zero-day leaves Microsoft Defender stuck in the past

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
BigDiskBuster leaves Microsoft's antivirus running but unable to install updates Serial Microsoft zero-day leaker NightmareEclipse has found another way to mess with Windows Defender, this time by stopping the antivirus from updating itself. The security researcher, also known as Abdelhamid Naceri, released a proof-of-concept dubbed “BigDiskBuster” that is designed to prevent Microsoft Defender Antivirus from installing platform and security intelligence updates. “Made a funny tool, completely denies defender from updating so you're stuck with your current version if the tool is running in the background,” NightmareEclipse said. The researcher describes BigDiskBuster as similar to their earlier “UnDefend” tool and claims it works on all supported versions of Windows, although they admit the current PoC is “a bit buggy and needs some rewritting [sic].” That compatibility claim has not been independently verified. The trick doesn't disable Defender. Instead, the PoC waits for an update to start, then tries to fill up the drive so there isn't enough space for it to finish. The code does this by creating hidden temporary files sized to consume the drive's free space, spinning up additional threads as needed to claim more. Once it detects that the Defender update has failed, it closes the files and returns the space. BigDiskBuster also opens Microsoft's Malicious Software Removal Tool executable, MRT.exe, in a way that restricts other processes' access to the file while the handle remains open. The result, according to NightmareEclipse, is that Defender stays stuck on its current platform and security intelligence versions as long as the tool keeps interfering with updates. A screenshot published alongside the PoC shows Windows Security reporting that a protection definition update failed with error 0x80070643. That's a generic installation error, however, and isn't evidence on its own that BigDiskBuster is at work. Leaving Defender stuck on old security intelligence is obviously less than ideal. The antivirus may still be running, but preventing it from receiving Microsoft's latest threat definitions could leave it less able to identify newly detected malware. The steady stream of bugs from NightmareEclipse comes amid a very public spat between the researcher and Microsoft over the company's vulnerability disclosure process. The researcher began dumping Windows zero-days and proof-of-concept code in April, claiming Microsoft had mistreated them and cut off their access to its vulnerability reporting system. Redmond wasn't exactly thrilled. In May, Microsoft criticized NightmareEclipse for releasing vulnerabilities without giving it a chance to fix them first, saying none of the initial bugs had been reported through its official channels. The company also invoked its Digital Crimes Unit, saying it would pursue cases against people engaged in malicious activity or enabling cybercrime – language widely interpreted as a threat of legal action against the researcher. That went down about as well as you'd expect with the security community. Microsoft subsequently walked back the rhetoric, saying it had “no intention to pursue action against individuals conducting or publishing security research.” By then, however, NightmareEclipse's earlier GitHub account had also been taken down, along with access to Microsoft's vulnerability reporting portal. The peace offering didn't end the feud. NightmareEclipse continued releasing Windows exploits, including RoguePlanet in June, LegacyHive in July, ShieldBreak in August, and ShieldCrash in September. Several of the researcher's earlier zero-days have since been patched by Microsoft, while some were exploited in the wild after their public release. BigDiskBuster is a rather different beast. Rather than providing an obvious route to SYSTEM privileges, it interferes with one of the basic things antivirus software needs: updating itself. There’s currently no indication that BigDiskBuster has been used in real-world attacks, and NightmareEclipse's claim that it works across all supported Windows versions remains unverified. Redmond has not responded to The Register's questions. In the meantime, its Nightmare apparently shows no sign of ending.® FineParser runs in a CPU-powered container, preserving tables and layout before handing documents to an LLM PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity BigDiskBuster leaves Microsoft's antivirus running but unable to install updates War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws Did Copilot write the synchronization code? Z.ai says sorry for slurping up your code, open sources ZCode
2026-09-22

Z.ai says sorry for slurping up your code, open sources ZCode

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws Chinese AI giant Z.ai has apologized after developers caught it pulling a Grok, packaging up and uploading user workspaces to cloud storage. In a case that’s highly reminiscent of the issues over which Elon Musk’s xAI was scrutinized in July, Z.ai’s code-generation harness wing, ZCode, was found packaging and git-encrypting entire user workspaces, including complete project histories, and shipping them off to Alibaba Cloud. Worse still, the private key used to decrypt the data was only held by the server under Z.ai’s control, meaning users could not access the files ZCode had uploaded, nor delete them. Ferstar, the researcher who first highlighted the issue, claimed there was no option for users to disable the behavior in their settings, and there was no disclosure of the practice in ZCode’s privacy policy. They said the core problem lay with the tool’s Repository Index functionality, which triggered the uploading of files after Repo Wiki generated pages in the cloud. ZCode released a statement on Monday apologizing for the “security issues” and confirming the data it uploaded had never been used to train its models. “We sincerely thank the community developers who previously identified issues in ZCode. Going forward, we will establish an ongoing product security vulnerability reporting and response process,” it Xeeted. “We welcome developers to continue reviewing ZCode and reporting potential issues, and we will provide rewards based on the severity of the issues reported.” ZCode said it tasked the China Academy of Information and Communications Technology (CAICT) and Beijing security company NSFOCUS to probe its product following the implemented changes. The company claimed the two outside assessments concluded that all the previously uploaded data has now been deleted and said the Repo Wiki feature was removed. ZCode also open sourced the entire project on GitHub, “placing the code under community scrutiny and making ZCode more open and transparent.” “Once again, we sincerely apologize and welcome continued scrutiny from the community. The full security assessment report will be released soon.” Ferstar confirmed the open sourced code showed no signs of the Repo Wiki still being implemented, but criticized the company for wiping commit records and the source code ZCode used to upload files pre-patch. For the uninitiated, Z.ai, formerly known internationally as Zhipu, is among the world’s AI heavyweights and one of the most heavily backed LLM-focused companies in China. It is the first AI company in the post-Gen AI era to launch and subsequently IPO on the Hong Kong Stock Exchange. Other Chinese AI giants are publicly traded, such as Alibaba and Baidu, but these were all established well before the AI era began. Z.ai is a startup with its roots in academic research. It spun out of Tsinghua University’s Knowledge Engineering Group research lab in 2019 and now develops AI models that it claims compete with the best in the West. Last month, the company claimed that its latest model, GLM-5.3, is as good as the most advanced equivalents developed by Anthropic and OpenAI at hunting for security vulnerabilities. Z.ai has also previously claimed the accolade of developing the first advanced model entirely on Chinese (Huawei) hardware. Meanwhile, the likes of Anthropic and OpenAI have reportedly expressed concern over the capabilities of models from Z.AI and Moonshot, while the US government mulls restricting access. ® FineParser runs in a CPU-powered container, preserving tables and layout before handing documents to an LLM PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity BigDiskBuster leaves Microsoft's antivirus running but unable to install updates War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws Did Copilot write the synchronization code? Privacy group slams EU for changing the data rules to cater to AI
2026-09-22

Privacy group slams EU for changing the data rules to cater to AI

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Schrems campaign raises specter of another legal challenge as legislators propose changes to GDPR The campaign group which forced the EU's highest court to strike down past US-EU data sharing deals has slammed the latest changes to European data protection laws designed to accommodate AI. Led by Austrian lawyer Max Schrems, None of Your Business (noyb) claims the proposal to alter the data protection legislation “in the context of AI” amounts to an abandonment of data protection principles. In September, the Commission said it would launch an “ambitious program” to strengthen the EU’s competitiveness and “radically lighten the regulatory load for people, businesses and administrations.” It proposed “immediate adjustments” to digital legislation to the boost competitiveness. Earlier, the EU had proposed changes in the legislation governing the processing of personal data, governed by the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR). “Where the processing of personal data is necessary for the interests of the controller in the context of the development and technical operation of an AI system … or an AI model, such processing may be pursued for legitimate interests,” a briefing note said. Noyb argues the amendments to Article 88c (original here) proposed by the European Commission (a leaked EU Council compromise draft renames it as article 88bis) would allow Big Tech to use all personal data collected over the past decades with virtually no restrictions, as long as this happens “in the context” of AI. Schrems said: “Under these proposals, the profits of AI companies would trump Europeans’ fundamental right to privacy. This is nothing but a digital expropriation of Europeans.” The campaign group said the changes could mean people who have never been customers of an AI company, whose data was entered into a system decades ago – for example in chats or on social media – may find their personal data is placed in the hands of an AI company. There is no need to ask users for consent: companies are automatically assumed to have an overriding “legitimate interest” if they train or use any AI product, the group said. Schrems added: “A likely majority of EU member states are now saying that the interests of Elon Musk, Marc Zuckerberg, Google or OpenAI, in making enormous profits, should take precedence over Europeans' fundamental right to data protection. This is nothing short of the ‘digital expropriation’ of Europeans. Everything we have ever entered into digital systems, or that AI corporations have otherwise obtained, becomes fair game for AI corporations to use.” The campaign group said the European Commission had abandoned its data protection priorities in the interests of the tech industry lobby. Meanwhile, the view of the European Parliament was mixed. However, the Court of Justice could examine whether the proposed changes can be reconciled with EU fundamental rights. “In the past, the European Court of Justice has struck down EU law in cases involving much less significant infringements of EU fundamental rights, such as data retention or the transfer of EU data to the United States,” it said in a statement. Noyb is famous for having successfully challenged and dismantled two major transatlantic data transfer pacts through the Court of Justice of the European Union: The Safe Harbor Agreement (in 2015, in the Schrems I case) and the EU-US Privacy Shield (2020, Schrems II). Schrems said the legal route might be the only option left to prevent the dilution of data protection law to satisfy the AI industry. “If the legislator has lost all sense of proportion and direction, then the people can only turn to the courts. Any extreme law that has a high risk of being overturned would at the same time only create more legal uncertainty – instead of promised simplification,” he said. ® FineParser runs in a CPU-powered container, preserving tables and layout before handing documents to an LLM PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity BigDiskBuster leaves Microsoft's antivirus running but unable to install updates War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws Did Copilot write the synchronization code? UK cops arrest 2 EvilTokens suspects, Microsoft seizes 50 phishing kit websites
2026-09-22

UK cops arrest 2 EvilTokens suspects, Microsoft seizes 50 phishing kit websites

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Used by crims to compromise 12K+ email inboxes across 10K+ global orgs A coalition of law enforcement and private-sector tech companies led by Microsoft have disrupted the EvilTokens phishing service, arresting suspected website admins, taking down more than 50 websites, and notifying victims of compromised email accounts. EvilTokens is a notorious Microsoft device-code phishing kit that emerged in February, and, within months of launching, had been used by criminals to compromise 12,000 email inboxes across more than 10,000 organizations worldwide. Like other similar phishing subscriptions, EvilTokens was sold as-a-service, and allowed buyers to bypass multi-factor authentication (MFA) and silently authenticate as the victim to the organization's Microsoft 365 applications. What made this one especially insidious, however, was its AI use. EvilTokens featured an AI chatbot that could analyze a victim’s inbox, and help criminals identify who to target, which trusted contacts to impersonate, and even which fraud strategies to use to maximize criminals’ paydays. “Since March 15, 2026, we have observed 10 to 15 distinct campaigns launching every 24 hours," Microsoft VP of security research Tanmay Ganacharya told The Register in an earlier interview about the phishing service. Late last week, in a coordinated effort that spanned the US and UK, Microsoft seized 50 websites used to operate the service and disabled more than 150 additional domains tied to its supporting infrastructure. Meanwhile, London’s Metropolitan Police Service on September 18 arrested two men, aged 32 and 38, who allegedly acted as the administrators of the EvilTokens website. Both men have been released on bail while the investigation continues. “Phishing services bring misery to thousands, taking money from everyday people across the world,” Detective Inspector Serena D'Adamo, whose team led the Met's investigation, told The Register in an emailed statement. “The Met remains committed to holding people to account who facilitate criminal enabling functions and think they can remain undetected.” Because healthcare organizations were among those targeted, Health-ISAC, a nonprofit that helps health sector organizations share cyber-threat information, joined Microsoft’s legal action as a co-plaintiff. After receiving authorizations from the US District Court for the Eastern District of Virginia, Microsoft and Health-ISAC worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, The Shadowserver Foundation and TRM Labs, took down EvilTokens’ platform, and Microsoft notified affected customers, helping them remediate compromised accounts. This action marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit’s (DCU) 40th court-authorized disruption over nearly two decades. According to Steven Masada, associate general counsel and DCU GM, this is also DCU’s first action against an end-to-end AI-enabled cybercrime service. “The infrastructure supporting EvilTokens has been disrupted, but the model it demonstrated will not disappear with it,” he said in a blog shared with The Register ahead of publication. “For organizations, the lesson is: assume that once an inbox is compromised, criminals may understand its contents in minutes, not days. Strong identity protections and monitoring remain essential, but organizations should also independently verify requests to change payment information, redirect funds or approve unusual transactions through a trusted second channel.” ® FineParser runs in a CPU-powered container, preserving tables and layout before handing documents to an LLM PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity BigDiskBuster leaves Microsoft's antivirus running but unable to install updates War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws China’s AI darling goes on the defense after engineer highlighted Grok-esque security flaws Did Copilot write the synchronization code? www.theregister.com - Articles
Who signed off on that AI agent? Nobody? Thought so.
2026-09-22

Who signed off on that AI agent? Nobody? Thought so.

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
SPONSORED FEATURE: AI agents may be unpredictable. Who they are, what they can do, and who owns them shouldn’t be. If you were in any doubt that AI agents are capable of complex autonomous work, that skepticism should have faded this summer. In July, news emerged that an autonomous swarm of OpenAI agents running in a sandbox broke out of it, of their own accord. Tasked with solving some challenges on an internal security benchmark, they worked out how to communicate with each other using the JFrog Artifactory package manager. The software then realized that they could use vulnerabilities in that software to gain internet access. Once they were out in the wild, they went into full goblin mode, finding exposed Hugging Face credentials and using them to get code execution access on several of the AI model's servers. Apparently OpenAI's agents have been busier still. While everyone else was on vacation this summer, they were also commandeering a German website and using it as a messaging board. Don't get us wrong; these agents weren't evil. They were just being the kind of employee you'd generally want: a self-starter with initiative. They were using all means at their disposal to accomplish the task they've been given. They just didn't know when to stop. OpenAI has since called the episode a "warning shot" for the industry, highlighting that governance is now a priority for anyone using agentic AI. These test agents were running internally and weren't supposed to have any safeguards. But the average company will want to keep its agents on a leash. What does that look like? A functional AI governance program depends on a full knowledge of what AI you're running, says Deepika Chauhan, chief product officer at DigiCert. She describes the pattern she sees at customer sites. "People may enable Claude or ChatGPT for their organization. They have visibility at that level," she says. "But visibility into how many agents I have? How many models do I have? How many MCP servers?" Not so much. "We haven't even started to attack the governance problem." This problem is growing. Three quarters of the 1,001 IT and cybersecurity decision-makers in DigiCert's 2026 AI Trust Pulse survey had deployed at least four AI-powered systems in the last six months. Around the same number had suffered from an AI-related security incident. Only half could trace AI decisions back to the models and data that produced them. Getting that visibility is the first step, Chauhan says. After that comes the actual management. The key here is to take baby steps. "Identify a small use case," she advises. One example might be to start managing agents that are involved in a particular workload or agents that you have built internally, as opposed to third party models. Perhaps predictably for a company that built its success on automated verification, DigiCert doesn't see agent management as a manual problem. "The sheer scale we are talking about and the technology required means that you can't have human intervention," Chauhan says. "One customer we were talking to was creating 300 to 400 agents a week. When you're working at that scale, it just doesn't work to have only manual controls." The other issue is that humans are fallible. Misconfiguration is a perennial bugbear in any IT environment, but it becomes particularly dangerous in an agentic AI situation. Other agentic SNAFUs at Meta and Anthropic illustrate the point perfectly. Both saw agents make their way onto the open internet when they shouldn't, and both were due to misconfiguration by a third-party company tasked with testing the agents. Traditional tools meant to manage human identities can't manage non-human identities well, adds Chauhan. Legacy identity and access management applications require people to approve access to different applications. There must still be a human in the loop, even if it's just for people to click an MFA approval button. Human employees might be willing to wait a minute or two for such approval, but agents talk to each other at machine speed. Instead, automated runtime attestation is key, managed by a robust central policy engine. That attestation relies on credentials and it's something that agents should carry with them, says Chauhan. This is one component in the company's AI Trust initiative. AI Trust is DigiCert's end-to-end governance framework that assigns identity automatically to AI entities, restricting them to safe, permitted actions while making them accountable. It uses cryptographic controls to ensure agent integrity, and the company has integrated it with existing infrastructure. The runtime attestation of AI Trust draws on the international travel metaphor in its approach. "We have a concept of an AI agent passport. There's an identity in the passport, but then that identity is recognized across any checkpoint anywhere in the world," she says, adding that the passport includes not just identity but access credentials (think of them like visas). Federation is key to this idea because, as we've seen already, agent interactions won't stop at the company boundary. "It's essential because you're literally going to have agents from company A talking to company B," she explains. DigiCert's whitepaper describes the concrete artifact: a tamper-evident passport cryptographically bound to a workload identity that encodes approved systems, permitted operations, authorized environments, data-sensitivity classifications, expiration states, and accountable human ownership. The scheme is anchored in DNS, the same mechanism DMARC uses to authenticate email senders, on the reasoning that every agent action begins with a DNS query. As agents get smarter, won't they be able to subvert these controls by thinking outside the box, Jason Bourne-style? After all, OpenAI's agents were able to break free of their sandbox to wreak havoc elsewhere. OpenAI's own post-mortem states that its models "are now powerful, persistent, and collaborative enough that, absent sufficient safeguards, they can find and exploit security weaknesses across multiple computer systems." Part of the problem here is that because agents are non-deterministic, you can't predict in advance what they're going to do. That problem becomes even more acute with newer frontier models like OpenAI's Astra, which saves tokens by internalizing a lot of its reasoning and not reporting its decision-making process in as much detail as previous models. The outer boundary can still be deterministic, even when the agents inside it aren't, says Chauhan. "You can black box what the agent is 'thinking' about or not thinking about, and what its agendas might be," she says. "But a deterministic boundary that says 'this agent can't access this thing', is your guardrail. That's a hard stop." Governance isn't just about technical guardrails, though. At some point, the question becomes organizational. When something goes wrong, someone has to put their hand up and own it. But most companies never assigned that ownership, Chauhan warns. She identifies three patterns in DigiCert's customer base. Some organizations put the existing IAM team in charge because they have experience governing service accounts. Others hand it off to the risk and compliance department. Another group will take a more holistic, multidisciplinary approach. This involves creating a 'tiger team' including representatives from network operations, the IAM team, and the security function. All of these executives will have a unique perspective on the issue. The third route seems to be the most productive because agents are going to be everywhere in your business. And a siloed approach runs the risk of being too restrictive. The surface area already touches every department that has dabbled in AI. Chauhan's advice on implementing AI Trust - get visibility, pick a small use case for enforcement, and then expand - is the foundation for effective AI governance, she says. That governance is in turn a critical component in fully realizing return on investment. "We must raise the urgency and awareness that this is table stakes for wider AI adoption," she urges. "You want to get all the benefits from AI, but what are organizations going to do if they're nervous about it? They're going to put a stop to some of the projects because of the risk involved." The headlines we're seeing about agentic transgressions are unnerving, but they're also in a unique category because they're research models from frontier providers. It seems unlikely that a regular publicly available agent would be quite so egregious today. However, we have also seen agents happily deleting files and even entire code bases because of internal flaws and humans who just waved their actions through. Organizations should be taking note of these events and laying the groundwork to avoid becoming headlines themselves. Working out who signed off on which agent and what that agent is allowed to do is a foundational skill that we can't afford to overlook. Saudi Arabia’s new Exobot EVs make the Cybertruck look normal
2026-09-22

Saudi Arabia’s new Exobot EVs make the Cybertruck look normal

The Verge | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
The new company is a joint venture between Saudi Arabia’s sovereign wealth fund and Foxconn. The new company is a joint venture between Saudi Arabia’s sovereign wealth fund and Foxconn. The Kingdom of Saudi Arabia is mostly known for its global dominance over petroleum production and oil reserves — not necessarily cars and auto manufacturing, and certainly not electric vehicle production. So it was a little surprising on Monday when a Saudi startup called Ceer unveiled a pair of hyper-angular, spaceship-like EVs, with plans to launch an additional five models by 2030. The Exobot (yes, that’s the real name) sedan and SUV are expected to go on sale in Saudi Arabia starting in 2027. The ultra-futuristic designs scream Tron, Blade Runner, and every other sci-fi movie you’ve seen — and then some. The gullwing doors are ripped straight from the Tesla Model X (which, of course, was ripped straight from the DeLorean and a range of other rare production cars). Wild and wedge-shaped, the Exobots seemed built explicitly to draw attention. Which, in an attention-based economy, is probably the right strategy. The sedan Exobot has low-slung, supercar proportions with a steeply raked windshield that flows seamlessly into the hood. The front fascia features a razor-thin, full-width LED light bar nestled under a sharp, forward-jutting nose, giving it a mean, visor-like glare. The two-tone bodywork and ample windshield immediately had me thinking about the Ferrari Luce, the polarizing electric supercar released earlier this year. The SUV boasts a blocky, military-meets-cyberpunk stance with high ground clearance and aggressive angles. The whole thing looks thick and armor-plated, with a flat roofline and a distinctive, tactical presence. I wouldn’t be surprised to see this thing bouncing along the Moon’s surface someday. Both were designed by Rob Melville, McLaren’s former design director. But according to Autocar, Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman had final say on the design. Ceer is hoping to take advantage of all the upheaval in the global car market, driven largely by the flood of Chinese electric vehicle exports in Asia, Europe, and South America. The US appears on its way to becoming an automotive backwater, with its ahistorical embrace of internal combustion engines and steep tariffs designed to keep Chinese EVs at bay. Why not release a pair of bombastic Batmobiles as a way to distract from the originating country’s extreme oil dependency? Ceer didn’t exactly emerge from nothing. Rather, it’s a joint venture between Saudi Arabia’s PIF sovereign wealth fund and Foxconn, the Taiwanese company most known for manufacturing the iPhone for Apple. The company will be funded by the kingdom’s vast oil wealth, but with the purpose of building a lineup of vehicles that run on electricity. Even though it’s distinctly a project built to jump-start the kingdom’s auto industry, Ceer needed to venture outside its borders when hiring for its top roles. Jim DeLuca spent 37 at General Motors, ending up as executive VP for global manufacturing, before joining Ceer as CEO. “We ⁠wanted to make sure the world could see what Saudi Arabia is capable of,” DeLuca told Reuters. The kingdom has had mixed luck when it comes to automotive projects. There were plans to build a Jaguar-Land Rover factory there over a decade ago, but to no avail. Toyota declined a deal with the Saudis in 2019. The Public Investment Fund owns a controlling stake in California-based Lucid Motors, which is now building a manufacturing facility in King Abdullah Economic City. Ceer is taking a diversified approach to its vehicle lineup. While the first models will be electric, subsequent vehicles will be hybrid and combustion-engine, depending on market demands. That said, starting a car company from scratch is daunting. Just ask Fisker, Lordstown, or Arrival — all of which went bankrupt. Even Lucid, with its support from PIF, is still struggling. We’ll have to see whether Ceer can buck that trend. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.
